  - Notes: **The first draft of this change would have opened every lifecycle gate.** It made
    `not_assessed` non-blocking; because `readLifecycleEvidence` stubs every field, that turns
    every refused stage transition into an allowed one — the "stub that waves projects through"
    the reader’s own header forbids, and LC-01 prohibits. An independent plan audit caught it
    before any code existed. The load-bearing test here is therefore not the label but
    `refuses every advanceable transition`, with a passing counterpart proving a complete
    snapshot still advances.

    `selectedDesignRef`, `manifestContentHash`, `proposedBy` and `approval` were ALREADY
    nullable, and null there means something specific ("not selected", "no approval"). A widened
    type cannot separate those from "nobody looked". It is also readable at runtime, which a
    TypeScript interface is not — the erased-type trap this phase’s plan retracted twice.

    `launch_ready` are `() => []` and permit unconditionally; `allocation_ready` and

    and the stub returned `[]`. The first pair is now asserted as gapless in a test named
    `PINS THE KNOWN HOLE`, so closing it (Phase 6 owns it) changes a test rather than passing
    unnoticed. The second pair is fixed by this change.

    gate-bypass mutation in the working tree.** Caught by hashing the file rather than
    assuming, reversed, and verified byte-identical against the recorded baseline. The harness

    **MY ATTEMPT-1 PATH SET WAS WRONG AND I FOUND IT MYSELF.** It cited 811/23 from
    hand-written globs (`src/services/lifecycle/__tests__/*.test.ts` plus
    `generation/__tests__/*.test.ts`) which silently omitted

    `approval-and-change-policy.md` §4.1 two phases ago. The real figure is 850/24.

    **SEVEN MUTATIONS, all detected, zero survivors**, each attributed by failing-test NAME and
    restored by sha256 against on-disk backups. Baselines
    `3e80e3c1…f4f418952` (`lifecyclePrerequisites.ts`) and `c352bfd2…076ce25b4c`
    (`lifecycleStatus.ts`), both verified identical after every mutation and at the end:

    - **M4** drop the `currentManifestRevision` guard → 2 fail: `THE SECOND GATE BYPASS: an
      unassessed currentManifestRevision must not permit the stage` and `and does not permit it
      one stage later either, through composition`.
    - **M5** drop the `proposedBy` guard → 1 fail: `an unassessed proposedBy is NOT_ASSESSED,
      never a fabricated self-approval claim`.
    - **M6** hand-write the assessed set, claiming three unmeasured fields → 1 fail: `the
      assessed set is DERIVED from the measurement map, not hand-written`. **This is the
      mutation that passed 811 tests in attempt 1.**
    - **M7** collapse `notAssessedGaps` to `filter(() => true)` → 2 fail: `excludes unmet gaps
      from a MIXED list` and `returns an empty list when nothing is unassessed`. **Also passed

    - M1/M2/M3 re-run: 4, 4 and 7 failures (up from 2, 4, 5 — the new tests widen the net).
  - Notes: **I introduced a gate bypass in the task whose entire purpose was removing gate
    bypasses.** `blueprint_approved` guarded `approval` and then read two more fields raw. A
    verifier probed it directly: with `approval` assessed and `currentManifestRevision` not,
    the placeholder is null, `null !== null` is false, the comparison is skipped and the stage
    emits ZERO gaps — it PERMITS, and `planning`/`building`/`operating` inherit it by
    composition. The stale-revision refusal that vanishes there is what LC-11 rests on. The
    `proposedBy` read was the other direction: `kind: unmet`, "No proposer recorded", a measured
    claim about a field nobody read — the same fabrication as `graphHasStart: false`, which this
    commit’s own header condemns, one predicate over.
  - Notes: **A value-based check could not have fixed the assessed-set defect, and that is the
    interesting part.** `[]` is a legitimate MEASURED value meaning "assessed, nothing wrong",
    so "declared measured but still a placeholder" is undecidable from the value alone. The fix
    had to be structural: derive the set from a measurement map so a field cannot be declared
    measured without a function that measures it. One list, not two that can drift.
  - Notes: The verifier also ran a positive control I had not: it added a field to
    `LifecycleEvidence` and confirmed `tsc` rejects the reader’s return literal (TS2741). So the
    acceptance chain is real, not asserted. Worth recording that the check I called "the
    enforcing link" was itself unverified until someone else tested it.

    pre-existing playwright `TS2307` set, zero new.

    **AGAINST REAL POSTGRES**, not only the doubles:

    ```
    refs_sha256 column: [{"column_name":"refs_sha256"}]
    refs indexes      : [{"indexname":"uq_blueprint_manifest_refs_delivery"},
                         {"indexname":"uq_blueprint_manifest_refs_student"}]
    verify (healthy)  : outcome success, {"tables":"7/7","indexes":"10/10",
                         "constraints":"6/6","columns":"1/1"}   EXIT=0
    verify (column dropped): outcome failure, error_class SchemaInvariantViolation,
                         {"columns":"0/1","indexes":"8/10"}      EXIT=1
    ```

    The dropped-column run also lost both indexes, which is correct Postgres cascade
    behaviour and is why the index count moves too.

    **SIX MUTATIONS, zero survivors**, each named, each restored by sha256:
    MC1 empty the column list → 3 fail. MC2 the assert stops asking → 1. MC3 the verify verdict
    stops counting → 1. **MC4 loosen the predicate to allow any `ALTER` → 1. MC5 stop requiring

    may only target a table this module OWNS`.
  - Notes: **This step LOOSENED a safety predicate, so its controls had to grow in the same
    change.** The old rule banned `ALTER TABLE` outright, which is over-broad against the
    repo’s own convention — **53** non-test files under `src/db/` use
    `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`, 91 counting `__tests__` — and which makes it impossible to
    add a column to a table that already shipped, since `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` is a no-op
    on an existing table. The positive control went from 4 cases to 12, covering every
    destructive `ALTER` shape plus `NOT NULL` without a `DEFAULT`, which does not merely risk
    something — it fails outright on a populated table.

    **The count in this bullet said 52 and the shipped tree is 53**, because I measured it
    before adding this task’s own `ALTER`. Re-derived WITH the positive control a count
    needs, since a grep over- and under-counts:

    ```
    grep -rl "ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS" src/db/*.ts | wc -l        # -> 53  (non-test)
    grep -rl "ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS" src/db/ | wc -l            # -> 91  (incl. tests)
    grep -rl "ZZZ_NOT_A_REAL_PATTERN" src/db/ | wc -l              # -> 0   (control)
    grep -rl "CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS" src/db/ | wc -l          # -> 110 (control)
    ```

    **And MC4/MC5 did NOT prove the loosening opened no door.** A verifier found that
    `ALTER TABLE x ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS c TEXT; DELETE FROM x` passed the narrowed
    predicate at 66/66 — a chained statement, which the pre-task blanket ban had refused for
    free. So did `ADD COLUMN … UNIQUE`, `… PRIMARY KEY`, `… CHECK (…)` and
    `… GENERATED ALWAYS AS (…) STORED`, each of which rewrites or locks a populated table for
    the same reason `NOT NULL` without a `DEFAULT` was already excluded. **The comment
    claiming "every destructive shape is still refused" was false when I wrote it.** Five
    controls and a semicolon clause added in attempt 2.
  - Notes: **A NEW property, not just a narrowed one:** an `ALTER` may only target a table in
    `REQUIRED_TABLES`. Shape-safety says nothing about blast radius, and altering a table
    another module created would make this file a second author of that schema — the
    cross-contamination the coordinator exists to avoid. MC6 proves it bites.
  - Notes: **Why `refs_sha256` and not `content_sha256`.** The latter is LC-10’s "exact
    version/hash" binding: `manifestContentHash` covers tenant + project + REVISION + refs and
    `approveBlueprint` writes it into `blueprint_approvals.content_sha256`. A
    revision-independent hash parked there could not bind an approval to an exact version. Two
    hashes answer two questions: one binds the approval, one identifies the write.
  - Notes: **The unique index refuses a revert, on purpose.** A later revision whose refs are
    byte-identical to an earlier one is rejected — correct, because identical refs mean there is
    nothing new to record, so a revert and a replay are indistinguishable by construction and
    returning the existing row answers both. `WHERE refs_sha256 IS NOT NULL` keeps the Phase 2
    rows, written before the column existed, from all colliding on NULL.
  - Notes: **A measurement error of mine, caught before it was published.** I first read the
    verify script’s exit code through a pipeline and got `exit=0` on the FAILING run — because
    `$?` reports the last stage of a pipe (`tail`), not the command of interest. Re-measured
    without the pipe: **1 when the column is missing, 0 when healthy.** Reporting the first
    reading would have been a false claim that a mandated post-deploy gate does not fail.
  - Notes: **THIRD time a `tsc` run has raced a mutation harness**, so the habit changes: `tsc`
    runs AFTER mutations, never in parallel with them. The cited run is on a tree whose three
    source files were first verified byte-identical to their pre-mutation backups.

    **This closes the third of the three dependencies Phase 4’s handoff named.** Its words
    were: "Production never CREATES an `operating_blueprint_manifests` row — it only reads and
    updates one," and the only two INSERTs in the repository were in tests.
  - Verification: **960 tests / 29 suites green.** The plan’s own preamble requires the
    invocation beside any aggregate and this entry shipped without one:

    ```
    # from backend/
    { find src/services/lifecycle -name "*.test.ts";
      echo src/routes/admin/__tests__/projectLifecycleRoutes.test.ts;
      echo src/scripts/__tests__/verifyProjectLifecycleSchema.test.ts;
      echo src/db/__tests__/ensureProjectLifecycleSchema.test.ts;
      echo src/services/delivery/__tests__/deliveryDesignLoop.test.ts; } | sort > /tmp/p.txt
    for p in $(cat /tmp/p.txt); do test -f "$p" || echo "MISSING: $p"; done
    DATABASE_URL=postgres://postgres:<scratch>@localhost:55432/lifecycle_scratch \
      node ../node_modules/jest/bin/jest.js --runInBand --runTestsByPath $(cat /tmp/p.txt)
    ```

    `tsc --noEmit`: 4 errors, the identical pre-existing playwright `TS2307` set, zero new —
    run strictly AFTER the mutation harness, per the habit change recorded in step 2.

    **SIX MUTATIONS, zero survivors** after the work below; each named, restored by sha256.
    MW1 drop `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` → 1. **MW2 collapse the two conflicts → 3.** MW3b the
    seam ignores its override → 2. MW4 key on the revision-dependent hash → 3. MW5 stop
    recording the proposer → 1. **MW6 cut the retry budget to one → 3.**
  - Notes: **THREE MUTATIONS SURVIVED THE FIRST ATTEMPT, and the reason was that my "race"
    tests were not racing the way I assumed.** `Promise.all` over the writer does NOT produce
    a revision collision: the connection pool serialises the `MAX(revision)` read, so no
    caller ever picks a revision another caller already took. The revision-conflict branch —
    **the branch the whole design turns on** — was unreachable, so collapsing the two
    unique-index conflicts (MW2) and cutting the retry budget to one (MW6) both cost nothing.

    Collapsing them is silent data loss: the loser of a revision race would return the
    winner’s row and its own blueprint would vanish. So it is not a candidate for removal, and
    Amendment 4 leaves one option — test it. That needed an injectable revision provider,
    which is the pattern this subsystem already uses: `verifyProjectLifecycleSchema` takes its
    `query` as a parameter "so a test can drive it with a doubled connection".
  - Notes: **MW3 was the other category.** Removing the cheap pre-read — an existence check
    before attempting the insert — cost zero tests, because the conflict path reaches the
    identical answer. Amendment 4 allows two categories, tested or removed, and an
    optimisation no test can observe is the second. Removed rather than declared expected.
  - Notes: **A test that named a branch it did not reach.** My first "A REVISION collision"
    test seeded revision 1 and asserted the writer landed at 2 — but 2 was free, so the first
    insert succeeded and the retry path never ran. A test is not about a code path because its
    title says so. Rewritten to force the collision, and then supplemented by the injected
    version once it turned out a real race could not reach it either.
  - Notes: **The positive control was FLAKY, which is worse than absent.** It dropped the refs
    index and raced two application calls expecting two rows; it passed once and failed once,
    because with the index gone whether the second caller’s pre-read sees the first row is a
    coin flip. A flaky control manufactures confidence intermittently and gives a verifier a
    different answer than the one published. Replaced with a DETERMINISTIC database-level
    control: two raw inserts sharing a refs hash at DIFFERENT revisions (so only the refs
    index can refuse), asserting `err.parent.constraint` is
    `uq_blueprint_manifest_refs_student` — because Sequelize reports every integrity violation
    as "Validation error" — then the same insert accepted once the index is dropped. Stable
    across three consecutive runs.
  - Notes: The integration suite SKIPS without `DATABASE_URL` and reports as **skipped**,
    which is visible; an ignored suite reports as nothing at all.

    **~~`Tests: 7 skipped, 7 total`~~ — THAT FIGURE WAS WRONG.** It was 9 when written and
    is 10 now that the tenant-scoping test exists. Re-measured, command and output:

    ```
    node ../node_modules/jest/bin/jest.js --runInBand --runTestsByPath \
      src/services/lifecycle/__tests__/manifestWriter.integration.test.ts
    # (no DATABASE_URL)
    # -> Test Suites: 1 skipped, 0 of 1 total
    #    Tests:       10 skipped, 10 total
    ```

    The entry two bullets above said "9 tests" in the same breath, so this contradicted
    itself on the page. A verifier caught it. **The number was never measured — I wrote down
    what I expected the suite to contain.**
  - Notes: **Not done here, and owned by T1.4:** populating `EVIDENCE_MEASUREMENTS`. The
    writer WRITES a manifest; reading it back into the evidence snapshot is the orchestrator’s
    job, and the plan assigns it there. That is also the moment the per-position binding
    control stops being precautionary and starts being load-bearing, because the measurement
    map becomes partially populated for the first time.

    `GENERATED ALWAYS AS (…) STORED`, each of which rewrites or locks a populated table for the
    same reason `NOT NULL` without a `DEFAULT` was already excluded. Closed, with five controls
    and a semicolon clause; the sentence is replaced by one that says the list is a record of
    shapes a verifier walked through, not a guess.

    ```
    # the test, in ensureProjectLifecycleSchema.additive.test.ts:
    #   "the token census over the statement list is what decides which bans can be global"
    const up = PROJECT_LIFECYCLE_STATEMENTS.map((s) => s.toUpperCase());
    const occurrences = (t) => up.reduce((n, s) => n + (s.split(t).length - 1), 0);
    expect(occurrences('PRIMARY KEY')).toBe(7);   expect(occurrences('UNIQUE')).toBe(10);
    expect(occurrences('CHECK')).toBe(6);         expect(occurrences('NOT NULL')).toBe(55);
    ```
  - Notes: **A \b CANNOT SURVIVE A SHELL, and it cost two attempts at one edit.** The quoted
    heredoc ate a backslash level, so python’s non-raw string turned `\\b` into a real
    BACKSPACE byte and shipped `/<0x08>DROP<0x08>/` — a regex matching nothing. The entire
    global destructive check was inert and **the suite read `34 passed`**. `sed -i s/\x08/\\b/g`
    then ate the backslash a second time. An inert regex is the same class of nothing as an
    inert mutation, and it reported as a pass both times.

    Fixed in the tooling, not by being careful: no backslash is typed through a shell any more.
    The patch scripts use `\` as a placeholder and substitute `chr(92)` on the way out, assert
    zero `0x08` bytes in what they wrote, and assert the escape text is present by name. The
    repo already knew this one — `reference_shell_heredoc_writes_real_backspace_for_backslash_b`
    — and the remedy it records (write python with the Write tool) is what finally worked.
  - Notes: **The mutation harness printed SURVIVED over three real detections.** `subprocess`
    with `text=True` decodes using the host locale, which is cp1252 here, and cp1252 has no
    U+25CF — so every failing-test NAME came back as `?` and the parser extracted nothing while
    the totals plainly read `1 failed`. Attribution by name is the entire point of the harness,
    so a count saying "failed" with no name parsed is now a **PARSER FAILURE** that exits
    non-zero. Reporting it as SURVIVED is the error the harness exists to prevent.
  - Notes: **One positive control for a whole predicate is not attribution.** All three
    `isAdditive` mutations were detected, but MA1 and MA2 reported the SAME test name — which
    says something broke without saying what. Standing rule 11, so the fix is structural: one
    test per operand class. The result also narrows an overstatement of mine, because MA1 kills
    only TWO of them — the column-rewriting ALTERs are refused by the prefix match, not by the
    global check:

    ```
    MA1  global check made inert  -> FAILS: rejects a chained SECOND statement, whichever branch the first matched
                                  -> FAILS: rejects a table-level action chained onto an ADD COLUMN
    MA2  ADD COLUMN count check   -> FAILS: rejects a mixed ADD COLUMN list where only the FIRST is guarded
    MA3  PRIMARY KEY hoisted      -> FAILS: every statement is CREATE ... IF NOT EXISTS (no ALTER, no DROP, no TRUNCATE)
                                  -> FAILS: POSITIVE COUNTERPART: the permitted forms pass, so the predicate refuses more than nothing
    ```
  - Notes: **A test of mine could not fail, under a comment claiming it could.** The
    revision-independence test asserted `Object.keys` of a literal it had just built; a verifier
    added a parameter and hashed it, and 963 of 963 passed. Rebuilt to vary the input the
    function actually receives, with a positive control so it does not also hold for a constant
    function. `MW-C` makes the hash depend on the whole input object — the implementation the
    test must refuse — and it now **FAILS: does NOT depend on a revision**, where before it
    survived.
  - Notes: **NOTHING typechecks the test files, and pointing tsc at them found two real
    errors.** `backend/tsconfig.json` excludes `**/*.test.ts` and `**/__tests__/**`, and

  - Notes: **I disagreed with the named fix on two of the five survivors, and the disagreement

    ```
    TRUNCATE      REMOVED   unreachable as a sole refuser: a bare TRUNCATE fails the branch
                            match anyway, a chained one dies on the semicolon clause. Its
                            control STILL PASSES after removal, which is the proof.
    USING         REMOVED   not merely dead - actively wrong. It REFUSED
                            `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS i ON t USING gin (c)`, a legitimate
                            additive statement. Every destructive USING also carries
                            ALTER COLUMN or ADD CONSTRAINT and was already refused.
    ALTER COLUMN  KEPT      load-bearing and merely UNTESTED. Deleting it as "dead" would
    RENAME        KEPT      have opened a hole: `ALTER TABLE t ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS c
                            TEXT, ALTER COLUMN d TYPE INT` passes the prefix, balances the
                            count and carries no banned constraint, so the global ban is the
                            ONLY thing refusing it.
    ```

    the two retentions by a **named failing test**. Amendment 4 has two categories and both
    are now used honestly, instead of one label stretched over four different situations.
  - Notes: **MD-1 is the one that mattered, and it is a production predicate.** `tenant_id = $1`
    in `findByRefs` survived deletion at 10/10 green on a fresh database, **under a comment
    whose first line named that exact mutation**. Removing a tenancy predicate is a security
    regression even when currently unobservable, and this subsystem has a standing rule that row
    checks come AFTER an audited tenant guard — so it is isolated, not deleted.

    **Why the obvious test could not see it.** Planting a row under another tenant with the same
    refs hash is not enough: the writer INSERTs first and the refs index is per-tenant, so the
    insert succeeds and the lookup is never reached. The fixture therefore forces a revision
    collision through the `nextRevisionFor` seam, which makes the insert conflict and the replay
    lookup run:

    ```
    with the predicate     lookup matches nothing -> retry -> budget exhausted -> THROWS
    without the predicate  lookup matches the OTHER tenant row -> resolves, created:false,
                           and a manifestId belonging to someone else

    BASELINE on lifecycle_md1_base   11 passed, 11 total
    MUTANT   on lifecycle_md1_mut     1 failed, 10 passed
      FAILS: the replay lookup is TENANT-SCOPED: it will not return another tenant’s row
    restore verified by md5 c76920380ab4118956f2981d94be86ed
    ```

    Both databases created from a template and dropped afterwards, with a zero-stray-index check
    on each — because the suite runs DDL, and a file restore is not a system restore.
  - Notes: **My own sweep found a SIXTH survivor the grading did not.** Loosening the
    `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` prefix to any `ALTER TABLE` survived all 20 other assertions,
    because the count check passes VACUOUSLY when a statement contains no adds at all: zero
    equals zero. So `ALTER TABLE t OWNER TO someone_else` and
    `ALTER TABLE t ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` would both have been accepted as additive. Two of
    the three new control shapes are genuinely dangerous. Final sweep: **15 operands, 0
    survivors.**
  - Notes: **The census figure was wrong twice before I measured it.** `7, 12, 12 and 53` was

- [x] P5-T1.3 attempt 4 FAILED 7/12 — my own fix had opened a destructive hole, and the
  structural answer to it (this entry claims NO score and NO pass)
  - Date: 2026-10-07
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: the ALTER branch of `isAdditive` rebuilt as an ALLOW-LIST over
    comma-separated actions; two no-op regexes repaired and their dropped assertions restored;
    the last shared-name control split four ways; one dead operand removed with its premise
    pinned by a test; the self-staling grep count replaced by its command.
  - Verification: **991 tests / 30 suites passed**, every path existence-checked. **14 operand
    mutations, ZERO survivors**, each killing its own named test; the removed operand proven
    absent from code rather than mutated. A **1,373-statement differential** against the
    pre-remediation predicate. tsc --noEmit: 4 errors, the identical pre-existing playwright TS2307 set, zero new, run after every mutation. Scoped typecheck of the seven lifecycle test files: exit 0, 0 errors.
  - Notes: **THE FIX I SHIPPED LAST TIME OPENED A DESTRUCTIVE HOLE.** In PostgreSQL `COLUMN`
    is OPTIONAL — `ALTER [COLUMN] col [SET DATA] TYPE ty [USING expr]` — so this carries
    neither `ALTER COLUMN` nor `ADD CONSTRAINT`, is valid SQL, rewrites a populated column,
    and my predicate ACCEPTED it after I removed the `USING` ban:

    ```
    ALTER TABLE t ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS newc TEXT, ALTER d TYPE INTEGER USING d::INTEGER

    a verifier ran it against real Postgres:
      a TEXT column holding 7.9 and 3.2 became an INTEGER column holding 8 and 3, exit 0
    ```

    **The comment justifying the removal was the whole justification, and it was false**:
    "every destructive `USING` also carries `ALTER COLUMN` or `ADD CONSTRAINT`". So `USING`
    was Amendment 4 **category 1** — a real operand needing a control — filed by me as
    **category 2**. That is standing rule 15, loosening a safety check without growing its
    controls, committed INSIDE the remediation that quotes standing rule 15.

    I had also told the grader to look hardest at exactly this, and it did, and it was right.
  - Notes: **THE REAL LESSON: the deny-list was the wrong shape, and I kept patching it.**
    Three attempts, three missed shapes — the chained second statement, then four constraint
    forms, then the keyword-less rewrite. Each time I added the keyword a verifier named and
    declared the class closed. **A deny-list over SQL is complete only by enumeration, and I
    was wrong about the grammar twice.**

    So the ALTER branch is now an ALLOW-LIST over its comma-separated actions: every action
    must be an `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS` carrying no constraint. `ALTER d TYPE`, `OWNER TO`,
    `SET SCHEMA`, `CLUSTER ON`, `INHERIT`, `VALIDATE CONSTRAINT`, and any spelling I have not
    thought of are refused **for not being on the list**, not because someone remembered to
    ban them. Complete by construction instead of by enumeration.
  - Notes: **THE DIFFERENTIAL IS THE EVIDENCE, and it is what was missing before.** A claim
    quantified over an input space ships with a generator and the enumerating command
    (standing rule 1). Both predicate bodies are EXTRACTED FROM THE FILES, never retyped, so
    the harness cannot drift from what ships:

    ```
    # from the worktree root; corpus built from components, not hand-listed
    python <scratch>/differential.py
    #   CORPUS 1373
    #   ACCEPTED NOW, REFUSED BEFORE (3):
    #     CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS i ON t USING btree (c)
    #     CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS i ON t USING gin (c)
    #     CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS i ON t USING btree (c)
    #   REFUSED NOW, ACCEPTED BEFORE (58)
    ```

    The "opened" column reduces to exactly the three legitimate index shapes the `USING` ban
    was wrongly refusing. **No destructive statement is accepted that the previous predicate
    refused** — which is the property I asserted last time without measuring.

    It also found a SECOND latent bug in the old version:
    `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS a TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'x', ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS b TEXT
    NOT NULL` passed, because a whole-statement `DEFAULT` check saw a DEFAULT somewhere and
    accepted an action that had none. Per-action checking fixes it, and a test pins it.

    the ten collections already has a validator owning its deep contract, and every one fails
    CLOSED on a malformed argument — measured, because the composition fixtures use exactly
    that. A Zod schema mirroring those contracts would be a SECOND definition of each, free to
    drift, and the drift would surface as a route 400ing on a payload the validators would have
    accepted. This layer guarantees container KIND only.
  - Notes: 422 rather than 400 when the blueprint refuses: a 400 says the request was malformed,
    and the request was fine while the BLUEPRINT is not ready — a different thing for a client
    to act on. And a project-id mismatch between URL and body is REFUSED rather than reconciled,
    because composing project B under project A’s URL would file B’s blueprint against A, and
    picking either would be guessing which the caller meant.

    than worked around.** `Measurement` is `(row: LifecycleRow) => unknown` and `LifecycleRow`
    is five columns; none of the ~25 evidence fields is derivable from them. The evidence lives
    in the manifest `refs_json` the new route feeds, and two fields map onto it EXACTLY
    (`requirementsWithoutProvenance` ← sources with a null `provenanceKind`;
    `unresolvedSourceBlocks` ← sources in state `open`). `requirementCount` deliberately NOT
    mapped to `sources.length`, because `SourceRef` is documented as "a requirement OR OTHER
    captured statement" and equating the two is the kind of plausible mapping this phase keeps
    being caught on.

    The blocker is small and concrete: `LifecycleRow` carries no project id, so the manifest
    cannot be located from it. **Populating the map with measurements that return empty would
    be the worst available state** — `assessedFields` would claim those fields were measured,
    every predicate would trust the empty value, and the NOT_ASSESSED refusal that currently
    blocks would become a PASS. That is the gate bypass the T1.1 carry-forward named, and
    shipping it to make a task look finished is the trade this phase has refused four times.

    `tsconfig.json` excludes `**/__tests__/**` and ts-jest strips types, so a green suite
    proved nothing about it. `workspaceBindingChecks` refuses both a blank `deepLink` and a
    falsy `preservesNavigationState`, so the drifted shape was one that module would reject.

    **Standing rule 14, in a file header**: a mechanism asserted without being measured, when
    the measurement was one scoped `tsc` invocation away. It is one definition now rather than
    a claim of one — `grep -c` for local helper definitions in `designSelection.test.ts`
    returns **0**.
  - Notes: **10/12 with no criterion at zero is the best grading in this phase**, and the first
    without one. The grader also confirmed the new assertion is falsifiable in BOTH directions
    — mutating only the fixture’s `visualContractRevision` to `null` fails the same test — so
    `acceptedDesign()` genuinely yields `issues: []` and a non-null `selected`, and the
    replacement for the tautology is not a differently-shaped tautology.
  - Notes: **MY RECOMMENDATION DIFFERS FROM T1.3, deliberately.**

    **T1.3: accept the block.** Five gradings — 8, 9, 7, 7, 8 — with a criterion-6 zero in
    four, and one of my remediations shipped a predicate that destroyed a populated column.
    There is no convergence, and the cap exists for exactly that.

    **T1.4: one further attempt is worth it, and the trajectory is the argument.** 8 → 8 → 10,
    zeros gone. The remaining fix was a mechanical refactor — move three helpers, restore two
    fields — not a new design, and it is already done and measured. A fourth attempt would be
    banking work that exists rather than attempting the task again.

    **Either way I am not raising the cap myself.** That distinction is the only thing that
    makes a cap a gate, and this phase has leaned on it five times already.

    `delivery_change_requests` already exists but is keyed to `delivery_project_id` alone — no
    `student_project_id`, no `tenant_id`, no uniqueness for a resubmit — so it cannot serve the
    student half without new DDL on a shared delivery table. The lifecycle already persists
    `condition` + `condition_reason`, and `awaiting_input` was already in `LIFECYCLE_CONDITIONS`:
    it IS "changes requested, waiting on the author". A change request is therefore a STATE, not
    an event, so the spec's resubmit-idempotency item is true by construction rather than by a
    uniqueness constraint that could be forgotten — and it avoided reopening the additive-DDL
    surface that cost T1.3 five gradings.
  - Notes: **The reason is STRUCTURED text, because the value could not carry the distinction.**
    Free text in `condition_reason` cannot tell "changes requested on r3" from the same words on
    r4, so a resubmit against a new revision would read as a duplicate and be dropped silently.
    `CHANGES_REQUESTED r<n>: <text>` plus a parser fixes that structurally (standing rule 11). The
    round-trip is asserted over generated texts including colons, newlines, empty string, and a
    text that itself looks like the format.
  - Notes: **THE KEYSPACE CANNOT COME FROM THE TYPE, and the plan cited it twice.** `refs_json` is
    declared `unknown` (`OperatingBlueprintManifest.ts:43`) and `ManifestRefs` is an interface, so
    "the manifest type's runtime shape" has no referent. The diff walks `emptyRefs(origin, id)`,
    whose contract is that every array is present.

    draft filtered top-level array keys — which returns *exactly* `INTEGRITY_CHECKED_LISTS`, making

    and both `trackMappings` lists never diffed at all. Moving a process from a runtime agent to a
    builder agent is precisely the change a reviewer must see, and it lives entirely in those
    nested lists. Caught by measuring the derivation instead of trusting it.

    asserting `changed === true` for an add/remove (the agent move) has BOTH present, so neither
    operand was isolated; dropping `changed` from `hasMaterialChange` survived because no test
    asserted it was true for a readable change. Five tests added, one isolating control per
    operand (Amendment 4), and all three then died by name.
  - Notes: **I reported the first mutation run as clean when it was not.** The run's notification
    said exit 0; that was `tail`'s exit code, because I piped the harness through it — the exact
    `$?`-through-a-pipe trap. The harness's own exit was 1 and it printed three survivors. Redirect,
    never pipe, when the exit code is the evidence.

    `.value` on a controlled textarea does not move React 18's internal value tracker, so the

    submission failed; the two that asserted a BLANK textarea refuses to submit passed without
    ever exercising `trim()`. The native value setter fixed both halves.

    Moving `log`/`redactPayload`/`correlationOf` from `routes/admin/` up to `routes/` left

    shared route helpers — NOT to dodge `lint-route-auth`, which does a non-recursive read of
    `routes/admin` and requires a guard name in every file it finds, so a guard-free helper
    dropped there would fail it.

    The frontend split exports `LIFECYCLE_API_BASE` so the `/api` prefix keeps ONE definition — a
    second copy is a second place it can go missing, and a missing prefix returns `index.html`
    with a 200 no mocked test can distinguish from a real response.

    response is JSON and is cast, so a server on a different build can hand the page a `state` it

    `routes/lifecycleRoutes.tsx` gates the change-request panel on the served list;

    an identical git status with a 30/30 post-restore run; backend tsc error set identical to
    the 4 baseline playwright TS2307 and zero new; frontend tsc exit 0; lint-route-auth OK
    across 132 admin route files.
  - Notes: **NO NEW ROLE AND NO NEW LOGIN.** A persona is a VIEW over `DELIVERY_ROLES`, and every
    membership is derived from `ROLE_GRANTS` through `rolesWithDeliveryPermission` — the inverse
    P5-T2 added — so the mapping and the grant table cannot disagree. The mapping is published as
    an explicit list with the command that re-derives it, because an earlier plan cycle got this
    vocabulary wrong in both directions.
  - Notes: **A PERSONA IS DEFINED BY A PERMISSION, NOT BY ITS NAME.** `DESIGN_REVIEWER` sounds
    like the designer and `ARCHITECT` does not sound like an author; both readings are wrong.
    Each persona is pinned to the permission that makes someone that persona and the roles fall
    out of the table.
  - Notes: **THERE IS NO `design.write` PERMISSION.** The vocabulary has `design.read`,
    `design.comment` and `design.approve` and nothing else, so design AUTHORSHIP is not an
    authority this system models. The `designer` persona is therefore defined by `design.approve`
    and the absence is named — inventing a permission to make the persona tidy would have been
    one the server never checks, offered by a UI that believed it existed.
  - Notes: **FOUR OF THE THIRTEEN ROLES BELONG TO NO PERSONA**, each excluded with a reason:
    mentor, QA reviewer and security reviewer are assurance roles, and client reviewer sits
    deliberately between viewer and designer — it holds `design.comment`, which is a write, so it
    is more than a viewer, and lacks `design.approve`, so it is less than a designer. Coverage is
    asserted BOTH ways, because a subset assertion cannot detect a role nobody covers, which is
    the under-coverage that let an earlier cycle ship a nine-role answer for a thirteen-role table.
  - Notes: **THE UI WAS DISAGREEING WITH THE SERVER, and the test for item 6 is what found it.**
    P5-T3 offered the change-request panel to every viewer; an observer's submit came back 403.
    Fixed by serving `permittedActions` from the status endpoint and gating the panel on it. The
    answer is computed server-side on purpose: a client-side copy of the role-to-permission table
    would be a second definition of the grant table, and the drift is exactly this bug.
  - Notes: **`ACTION_PERMISSION` holds no literal.** Each entry is the constant the acting code
    already checks — `STAGE_PERMISSION.blueprint_approved`, `CHANGE_REQUEST_PERMISSION`,
    `STAGE_PERMISSION.requirements_ready/design_ready/building` — so changing the server moves the
    matrix with it instead of leaving one that quietly disagrees.
  - Notes: **I expected a delivery owner to hold every gated action and the grant table refused
    it.** An owner has `story.write` and `story.review` but not `story.execute`; only BUILDER and
    ASSOCIATE_BUILDER can execute. The table was right and the expectation was wrong, and the
    corrected test now asserts the separation in both directions rather than just the four.
  - Notes: **LC-14 is PARTIAL by design, and the fourth clause has a tripwire rather than a test
    that cannot fail.** Three clauses are evidenced per route over a source-derived route list:
    direct API bypass refused (every route 409s with the flag off, and guards balance per route in
    both files), cross-tenant read refused, forged approval refused — the approver comes from the
    session and the schema declares no field for it. The fourth, worker bypass, has NO PATH TO
    TEST: `approveLifecycleBlueprint` has exactly one caller and it already runs the audited write
    guard. A worker-bypass test would assert that a nonexistent job cannot do something. Instead a
    tripwire asserts the importer set is exactly the definition and the one route, derived by
    walking the tree — so ADDING a worker later fails it. Proven by writing a synthetic importer:
    the tripwire tripped by name, the file was removed, and `git status` matched before and after.
    The live refusal is owed by P6-T1.
  - Notes: **Two measurement hazards found and recorded, both mine.** A mutation harness poisoned
    the ts-jest cache, so a later run reproduced a mutant that was no longer on disk — proven by
    `sha256sum` matching the committed blob while one test failed, and by `--no-cache` passing.
    All three harnesses now pass `--no-cache`, and T3's and T4's committed evidence was re-run
    under it rather than assumed unaffected. Separately, a jest run launched alongside a harness
    read the target mid-mutation and failed two unrelated tests; this repo had already recorded
    the tsc version of that hazard and I repeated it with jest.

    carriage return, so `$` never matches. The sweep went on reading prose as code while LOOKING
    repaired. It surfaced only because the new positive control — assert `/compose` IS flagged when
    the exclusion is removed — came back empty. A no-op strip is worse than no strip: it looks like
    a control. Third CRLF trap of this session.

    reads an unvalidated cast, so a frontend deployed ahead of its backend — a shape this repo has
    shipped — would hit `undefined.includes` and white-screen. A test now asserts a payload missing
    the field withholds the action and leaves the rest of the page intact.

    exceeds CLAUDE.md's 500-line ceiling, which the table does not exempt tests from. But six test

  - Date: 2026-10-09

  - Verification: 14/14 passing, `jest --no-cache --runTestsByPath`, exit 0. Backend
    `tsc --noEmit` exit 2 with exactly the 4 documented `TS2307 Cannot find module 'playwright'`
    errors and **zero new** — baseline clean. Tripwires PROVEN to trip: a synthetic probe file
    containing a raw `INSERT INTO delivery_projects`, a `bulkCreate` and a `hooks: false` made 4
    tests fail by name; removing it returned the suite to 14/14 and `git status` to only the three
    intended paths.
  - Notes: **The chokepoint approach was abandoned on measurement.** The discovery record claimed
    three chokepoints "cover every row"; they cover 6 of 7 read as categories and 5 of 7 as
    enumerated. The missed site, `services/factory/govDeliveryProject.ts`, landed three days after
    the inventory and is LIVE — so a delivery project has been created with no lifecycle on every
    government pursuit approval since 2026-10-04. Registration is now on the model, following
    `services/workLedger/ticketCreationLedgerHook.ts`, which solved the same problem on `tickets`.
  - Notes: **The precedent's SAFETY contract was deliberately inverted, because its preconditions
    do not hold here.** `ticketService.ts` contains "transaction" zero times, so its hook can write
    on any connection and swallow its errors harmlessly. Both live delivery-project creations run
    inside explicit transactions and `project_lifecycle_states.delivery_project_id` is a real FK,
    so a hook copying that precedent could not see the uncommitted parent, would trip the FK, and
    would swallow the failure — leaving exactly the unregistered project LC-01 exists to prevent.
    This hook forwards `options.transaction` and lets errors propagate, so a failed registration
    aborts the create. `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` is what keeps gov re-approval idempotent under that
    contract. A test asserts the hook contains no `catch`, so the inversion cannot be "hardened"
    away by a later reader.
  - Notes: **Student projects are deliberately NOT registered, and it is escalated, not deferred
    quietly.** `project_lifecycle_states.tenant_id` is `NOT NULL REFERENCES tenants(id)`, and
    `models/Project.ts` has no tenant column — nor do `Enrollment`, `Cohort`, `ProgramBlueprint`,
    `PlatformIdentity`, `UserCurriculumProfile` or `AdminUser`. `tenantResolver` resolves only from
    request-scoped inputs, and a model hook has no request. So LC-01's student half is unbuildable
    today; raised to the DRI, who chose to add `tenant_id` to `projects` and backfill. Recorded in
    `tmp/escalation.json`. LC-01 is claimed for the delivery half only until that lands.
  - Notes: Three self-inflicted defects were caught by controls rather than review, and each one is
    the reason a control exists. The sweep's first run counted this hook's own header comment as
    two creation sites, so it now strips comments — with a positive control, because this
    programme's last comment-stripper silently no-opped on CRLF. The `no catch` assertion initially
    read the unstripped file and so failed on its own documentation. And the first pre-filter used
    the literal `'INSERT INTO'`, which would have hidden `Insert Into` and `INSERT  INTO` from a
    case-insensitive pattern. Suite time fell from 105s to ~150ms of actual sweeping after the
    read-and-strip was cached once.

- [x] P6-T1a (part 2 of 2) — the registration hook's write-feasibility contract, measured against
      a real Postgres
  - Date: 2026-10-09
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: new `__tests__/lifecycleRegistration.integration.test.ts` (11 tests), plus two
    corrections to `projectLifecycleRegistration.ts`'s header that the measurements forced.
  - Verification: 11/11 against Postgres 15.16 in a throwaway container, and 25/25 across both
    T1a suites together (`jest --no-cache --forceExit --runTestsByPath`, exit 0). The table under
    test is built by `DeliveryProject.sync()` rather than hand-written DDL, so the hook is
    exercised against the real column shape instead of a stand-in that could drift.
  - Notes: **The design's load-bearing assumption HOLDS.** Registration writes from inside the
    caller's transaction and the FK to the still-uncommitted parent row resolves — asserted by
    showing the lifecycle row visible within the transaction and invisible outside it, which is
    what distinguishes "joined the transaction" from "worked because the parent had already
    committed". A rolled-back create leaves neither row.
  - Notes: **The abort-on-failure claim was WRONG as first written, and the test says so in its own
    name.** It holds only when the caller opens a transaction. Without one the project INSERT has
    already committed by the time `afterCreate` runs, so a throwing hook surfaces an error while
    the row survives unregistered. That is not hypothetical: none of the three seed scripts
    contains the word "transaction". So the honest guarantee is two-part — for the two live request
    paths, no unregistered project; for operator-run seeds, an unregistered project is DETECTABLE.
    `unregisteredDeliveryProjectsSince()` is what makes the second half true, and the test proves
    it finds both deliberate orphans.
  - Notes: **A positive control measured something better than it was written to measure.** It
    dropped `uq_lifecycle_delivery_project` expecting duplicate lifecycles to become possible;
    instead the INSERT raises, because `ON CONFLICT (delivery_project_id)` has no index left to
    infer from. So that index is load-bearing for registration *working*, not merely for it being
    idempotent — and since `ensureProjectLifecycleSchema` warn-skips rather than throwing, "the
    index is missing and nothing said so" is a reachable deploy state. That makes the post-deploy
    schema verification genuinely load-bearing rather than ceremonial.
  - Notes: `findOrCreate` fires the hook on its create branch and NOT on its find branch, measured
    rather than read off the Sequelize source — which matters because
    `businessProcessSeedService.ts:135` is a `findOrCreate` and no `.create(`-only sweep would ever
    have found it. `bulkCreate` does not fire it, confirming the gap the source tripwire guards.
  - Notes: A first run looked like a ten-minute hang. It had actually finished its assertions and
    written a complete report; the connection pool keeps the process alive even though `afterAll`
    closes the Sequelize instance. `--forceExit` is now in the documented command, and the lesson
    is to read the redirect file before concluding nothing ran.

  - Notes: **`/tmp` means two different directories here, and Python says the wrong one exists.**
    The line that was meant to add the typecheck evidence to the T3 commit message ran under
    Windows Python, which resolves `/tmp` to `C:\tmp` — while Git Bash maps `/tmp` somewhere else

    wrong one. The trap worth remembering is the guard: `os.path.exists('/tmp')` returns **True**
    under Python, because `C:\tmp` is a real directory — so checking for the directory before
    writing would have passed while writing to the wrong place. Every other Python edit this
    session used a repo-relative path, and the one file backup went through `cp` in Bash, so
    nothing else was affected. Rule: in this environment, never pass a `/tmp` path between Bash
    and Python — use the scratchpad's absolute path or a repo-relative one.

    documented playwright `TS2307`s, zero new, neither changed file named.
  - Notes: **The clip was real and genuinely silent.** `delimited` clips an over-budget brief or
    document and writes `…[truncated at N chars]` INTO THE PROMPT — informing the model and nothing
    else. A 250,000-character document loses 50,000 characters and the build looks normal.
    `request.md:243` forbids exactly this.
  - Notes: **It is the same defect Ali already rejected, in a different place.** The note in
    `buildDecomposeUserPrompt` records it: a tier band was sent as an instruction, a detailed brief
    came back missing requirements, and the answer was "I think we should build out all of the
    requirements - no matter how big … I would rather have all the requirements there." The
    requirement CAP was removed then. The source CLIP stayed, and this is it.
  - Notes: **DETECTS rather than refuses, deliberately.** A blocking overflow is the stronger answer
    and `request.md:243` permits it — but refusing above a budget would fail builds that succeed
    today, and how many is UNMEASURED: it needs the real distribution of brief and document sizes in
    production. Shipping a refusal on an unmeasured assumption is how a gate hurts the people it was
    built for, which is the same reasoning that keeps `release_without_user_visible_workflow`
    advisory. Logged `partial` rather than `success`, because the generation that follows is working
    from less than it was given. Promoting it to blocking is gated on that measurement.
  - Notes: The clamp itself STILL HAPPENS and is asserted alongside the report — it is a SAFE-002
    control against an unbounded prompt, not an accident, so neither behaviour can be traded for the
    other. One test asserts the report fires EXACTLY when the prompt carries a truncation marker, so
    the two can never disagree: a clip with no report is the original defect, and a report with no
    clip is a false alarm. Inputs are generated from the exported budgets rather than hand-sized, so
    raising a limit moves the tests with it.

    returned rather than refused, deliberately: `MAX_SHAPE_ATTEMPTS` is 2, and a hard local check
    would turn one model hiccup into a failed build for a student. It is safe because the failure
    direction is safe — an unfilled field is UNMEASURED and unmeasured BLOCKS. The gate degrades to
    "cannot tell", never to "fine". Asserted by its own test.

    not a desirable precursor.

- [x] P6-T7 (LC-14): the new seam's tenancy test, cross-tenant clause re-tested
  - Date: 2026-10-12
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `lifecycleRegistration.tenancy.integration.test.ts` (new, 13 tests) covering the
    registration seam T1a added. `sourceSweep.ts` (new) holds the shared sweep apparatus and
    `sourceSweep.test.ts` (new, 9 tests) is a control on that control. Also gave
    `sbpOrchestrator.autoPublish`'s first test an explicit 30s timeout, which closes the latent CI
    flake recorded as ladder ticket L9.3.
  - Verification: tenancy 13/13 against a real Postgres (database t7_tenancy); sourceSweep 9/9;
    lifecycleRegistrationCoverage 18/18 unchanged; 104 of 105 suites and 1784 of 1785 tests before
    the flake fix, 105 of 105 after.
  - Notes: A MUTATION FOUND A DECORATIVE CONTROL IN MY OWN SWEEP, before it shipped. Prefixing the
    audited guard call with an always-false condition left ALL FIVE source-sweep tests passing:
    the sweep checked that the call appears and that its index sits between the row lookup and the
    return, and a disabled call satisfies both. A source sweep can establish ORDER; only running
    the code can establish REACHABILITY. Fixed two ways: the call must now match from the start of
    its line (which kills the single-line form), and a new REACHABILITY block intercepts the module
    `loadAndAuthorize` dynamically imports and asserts the guard is actually invoked with the
    tenant read off the ROW. The mutation now dies to 3 tests instead of surviving.
  - Notes: THE ORDERING MATTERS FOR A CONCRETE REASON and the test says it. The row lookup is
    deliberately NOT tenant-filtered: unfiltered lookup then audited guard means a cross-tenant
    attempt is FOUND, DENIED and WRITTEN to the access ledger. A tenant-filtered lookup would
    return null, the caller would see not-found, and nothing would be recorded - a cross-tenant
    probe indistinguishable from a typo. Mutation A (adding a tenant filter to the lookup) is
    killed by the sweep.
  - Notes: I CORRECTED MY OWN CORRECTION. I claimed an earlier session had recorded the comment
    stripper's limitation wrongly as "a URL in a string is mangled". It had not:
    `lifecycleRegistrationCoverage.test.ts` states it precisely and correctly - the colon guard
    protects the SCHEME, but a LATER colon-less `//` in the same string is still blanked. My probe
    used a URL with no second `//`, so it survived and I read the whole claim as false. It was my
    compression of the finding that was lossy, not the finding. Both suites now assert the same
    precise boundary including the distinguishing `a//b` case.
  - Notes: BOUNDARY MEASURED AND RECORDED, not fixed. Moving a delivery project to another tenant
    by direct update does NOT move its lifecycle row: the hook records the tenant at registration,
    so the two drift. Nothing in the product reassigns a delivery project's tenant today, so the
    blast radius is zero and inventing a trigger for an unobserved operation would be a gate
    acquiring behaviour nobody asked for. Pinned by a test so closing it, or accepting it, is a
    deliberate change. Belongs in the Phase 6 boundary report (L6.11).

- [x] P6-T1b (LC-01, student half): projects get a tenant, and a lifecycle
  - Date: 2026-10-12
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `db/ensureProjectTenantSchema.ts` (new) adds a nullable `projects.tenant_id`,
    an index, a slug-resolved backfill and a post-condition assertion that checks the DATA as
    well as the catalog. `services/lifecycle/studentTenant.ts` (new) resolves the tenant.
    `projectLifecycleRegistration.ts` gained `studentProjectTenantHook` (beforeCreate) and
    `studentProjectLifecycleHook` (afterCreate) plus `unregisteredStudentProjectsSince`.
    `models/Project.ts` declares the column and wires both hooks. Wired into server boot before
    `ensureProjectLifecycleSchema`.
  - Verification: studentProjectRegistration.integration 9/9 on a fresh Postgres; 115 of 115
    suites and 1940 of 1940 tests across lifecycle, sbp, the schema module and the models;
    projectRoutes and enrollmentRoutes both pass with the hooks live; tsc at the 4-error
    playwright baseline.
  - Notes: DRI decision recorded. Ali chose `colaberry` on 2026-10-12 after asking what the
    backfill was and reviewing it: additive nullable column, reversible by dropping it, idempotent
    via `WHERE tenant_id IS NULL`, no behaviour change until Phase 8 activation, and zero
    ambiguous rows (all 62 projects carry both an enrollment and a program).
  - Notes: I SHIPPED A REFUSAL POLICY THAT WAS WORSE THAN THE PROBLEM, and the suite found it by
    ORDERING. The resolver originally refused to attribute a tenant whenever more than one tenant
    owned `projects` rows, which I had described to Ali as "refuse rather than guess". One test
    placed a project in a second tenant, and every test after it came out unattributed and
    ungoverned. In production that means ONE deliberate cross-tenant placement permanently
    disables attribution for every project created afterwards. A refusal does not prevent the harm
    it was aimed at: the harm is "filed under the wrong tenant", and the refusal converts it into
    "no lifecycle at all", which under enforcement means refused story generation. It breaks the
    working case to avoid a hypothetical one, which is the same over-refusal the source overflow
    and `release_review`'s advisory half were kept from. Now: it resolves, and logs a WARNING
    carrying both tenants.
  - Notes: THE HONEST LIMIT, recorded rather than engineered around. While the default stands, a
    second tenant genuinely running cohorts WOULD have its projects attributed to Colaberry. That
    cannot be distinguished from a deliberate placement using `projects.tenant_id` alone, and the
    real fix is deriving the tenant per project from its programme - which no table on that path
    can support yet. The warning plus `unregisteredStudentProjectsSince` is what makes the state
    visible; claiming a refusal solved it would have been worse than saying this.
  - Notes: The asymmetry with the delivery hook is deliberate and documented in both. The delivery
    hook THROWS on failure; the student hook does not. Setting a tenant is DATA, refusing to create
    a student's project is ENFORCEMENT, and throwing there would break a live creation path for a
    governance reason with the flag off. An unattributable project is created, left unregistered,
    and logged as a ContractViolation - and `storyAuthorization` refuses it authorized stories.
  - Notes: I wrote one test that asserted a literal array's length and nothing about the code, and
    caught it before running. Replaced with a real positive control: the multi-tenant warning does
    NOT fire in the healthy single-tenant case, so the signal is not unconditional noise.

    `stagesDriven` or `stagesNotReached`, and a not-reached entry REQUIRES a reason. That rules out

    values are `FACT` (3) and `DECISION` (3), and `req()` builds
    `{ dimension: 'requirements', classification: 'FACT' }`. It returned zero. Caught only because
    the test asserts `mustHave.length > 0` rather than checking the partition alone: an empty
    requirement list would otherwise have reported "nothing uncovered", which is absence read as a
    clean result.


## Entries recovered from PROGRESS.md, 16 October 2026

The eighteen entries below were appended to `PROGRESS.md` during this session and belong here.
`origin/main`'s CLAUDE.md states that `PROGRESS.md` is a **sealed archive** -- "read it for
history, never append to it" -- and that the per-session log is this file. The copy of CLAUDE.md
loaded into my context was the stale OneDrive one, which still describes PROGRESS.md as the hard
gate.

**This file's own first entry already recorded the correct rule** ("which is why the log is here
and not in the sealed `PROGRESS.md`"), so this is drift away from something I had established at
session start, not a rule I never knew. Found because
`scripts/generateSessionChangelog.js` reads `docs/sessions/<id>.md` and NOT `PROGRESS.md`, so the
session report had been silently missing every entry since P7-T1.1 -- it kept reporting 58
entries while the work went elsewhere. An independent verifier flagged the symptom (a regenerated
report containing zero cards for the packet); this is the cause.

The duplicates left in `PROGRESS.md` are flagged rather than deleted: it is a shared file and
removing eighteen blocks from it risks colliding with another session's uncommitted work.

- [x] **Corpus runner: `driven` becomes a per-stage measurement, and the traceability rule gets a gap it can report**
  - Date: 2026-10-13
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `backend/src/services/lifecycle/__tests__/corpus/corpusRunner.ts`, a new `corpusStages.ts`, and the suite. Rebuilt after the task verifier failed attempt 1 at 7/12 and attempt 2 at 9/12. Attempt 1's partition over the stage list was real, and every member of it was false: `driven` was ONE boolean (`compositionError === null`) fanned across all ten composed stages, so a case refusing at `agents` still reported `effort`, `design` and `complete` as driven while `partitionIsTotal` returned true. Each stage's outcome is now derived from the composition result. The pipeline has two halves with genuinely different rules, both read off `blueprintComposition.ts`: generation (`handoff` through `effort`) SHORT-CIRCUITS, so a stage after the refusing one never ran; the four surface checks (`workspaces`, `workspace_states`, `controls`, `design`) run UNCONDITIONALLY, which that file states in its own comment ("GENERATION FIRST, AND ITS REFUSALS ARE NOT FATAL TO THE SWEEP"). The refusing stage is read from `refusals[].stage` and not `draft.stage`, because `composeBlueprint` returns `draft: draft.refusals.length === 0 ? draft : null` and so nulls the draft in exactly the short-circuit cases that matter. `DECLARATION_SELF_SUPPLIED` gets its own branch: it returns `stage: 'agents'` before `buildSourceHandoff` is ever called, so the three stages that normally precede `agents` did not run either. Traceability is requirement-BLOCK coverage measured with the SHIPPED rule, `factoryValidate`'s `SOURCE_COVERAGE`; the three invented stage names are replaced by five real members of `LIFECYCLE_STAGES`; the not-reached reason names only `approveBlueprint` and `publishedPlanState`, the two functions that genuinely need a database; and requirement-to-story coverage is reported `null` rather than `[]`. Attempt 3 split the stage vocabulary into `corpusStages.ts`, because the runner had reached 13 exported symbols against CLAUDE.md's hard ceiling of 12, whose rule is that the next change MUST split before adding code.
  - Verification: every number here was measured AFTER the last source byte was written, in one consolidated run. `npx jest --config jest.ci.config.ts src/services/lifecycle/__tests__/corpus` reports **34 passed, 34 total**, 1 suite, 5.0s -- three orders of magnitude inside the plan's 8-minute corpus budget. SEVEN mutations each drive the suite RED, failing 6, 3, 3, 7, 1, 3 and 1 tests respectively, with both touched files restored byte-identically afterwards and sha256 re-checked against the pre-mutation baseline (`97677141...` and `9f7f7dc5...`, identical before and after all seven). M4 switches OFF the rule inside the shipped `factoryValidate` and fails 7, which is what proves the suite exercises the real validator rather than a copy of it; M1 restores attempt 1's constant `driven` and fails 6. `npx jest --config jest.ci.config.ts src/services/lifecycle/generation src/services/factory src/services/lifecycle/__tests__/corpus` reports **1083 passed, 0 failed tests**, 12 skipped, 1095 total; the 3 failed SUITES are all `Cannot find module 'adm-zip'` under `src/services/factory/proposal/__tests__/`, the already-classified local-only missing-dependency class, untouched by this change. The decisive test feeds it COMPLETE evidence and asserts the answer is NOT `discovery`; without that, a constant would pass everything. **WHAT THE WRITE-NOTHING PROOF ACTUALLY DOES**: snapshots row counts across all five tables the query reads, runs assess + preview, and asserts the whole map is unchanged - with a companion assertion that the preview produced proposals, so the equality is not vacuous. **A MEASUREMENT ERROR I CAUGHT BEFORE REPORTING IT:** re-measuring the plan 62 first gave 48, and I was one sentence from reporting drift. 48 is DISTINCT `build_intake` projects - the wizard subset - not the `projects` table. The stats collector is ASYNCHRONOUS; the grader had run it out-of-band around the suite, where the lag had settled. An assertion whose truth depends on a background flush is flaky, and shipping one would have been worse than the weaker check it replaced. (2) Replaced it with a per-table md5 CONTENT DIGEST — synchronous, no collector, sensitive to insert, update and delete alike — then tried to prove it mutation-sensitive and **the proof failed**: I had injected `SET created_at = created_at`, a no-op that changes no VALUE, so the digest was legitimately unchanged. and the cutoff boundary is exactly right — `assessLegacyProjects` uses `created_at < cutoff` while `unregisteredStudentProjectsSince` uses `>=` with the **same constant**, so the two partition the project set with no gap and no double-count, where `<=` would have put a project created at the cutoff instant in both reports. **CARRIED:** nothing pins that partition and it spans two files, so one test asserting the two queries are complements would make the coupling regression-proof; One theoretical note recorded: `string_agg(x, '|' ORDER BY x)` does not escape its delimiter, so two different row multisets could in principle join to the same string — `ROW::text` paren-wraps every element so a collision needs values containing `)|(`, unreachable here; `md5(string_agg(md5(x), '' ORDER BY md5(x)))` removes the delimiter entirely if it is ever wanted. **CARRIED, with the grader's agreement:** the module is now **316 lines**, past CLAUDE.md's ~300 soft target though well under the 500 ceiling — the next change to it is the natural split point and the two halves already separate along the read/pure line, and this is written down precisely because the T2 version of this note went unhonoured until a verifier caught a 501-line file; the `<` / `>=` partition against `unregisteredStudentProjectsSince` is still unpinned and spans two files; `as never` casts remain in the unit fixtures (harmless, since tsconfig excludes tests, and an override parameter on `completeEvidence()` would remove them); and the assessor has **no non-test caller**, so "opt-in" has no surface to opt in from — which the grader notes is also *why* the null-tenant premise error could never have surfaced from the production run.

Control: a nonexistent project id refused, so the gate is not bypassable. Root cause proven at runtime on a real instance: column `created_at` = `2026-04-02T17:33:42.613Z`, `project.created_at` = **undefined**, `project.get('createdAt')` = the Date, `'created_at' in attributes` = **false**, `'createdAt' in attributes` = **true**; the same guard against the real attribute evaluated **true**, which is the control proving the fix. **THE TEST REPRODUCES THE DEFECT:** reverting the production fix makes **exactly 2 tests fail** (`PERMITS a project that predates the registration hook`, `compatibility mode is PER PROJECT`), both expecting `legacy_compatibility` and getting a refusal; restored, **21/21 pass**. Regression sweep: `src/services/(sbp|lifecycle)/` → **157 suites, 3,032 tests passed, 0 failed** (15 DB-backed suites skip without `DATABASE_URL`). Backend `tsc` (5.9.3): **4 errors, all pre-existing TS2307 `playwright`**, unchanged baseline.
  - Notes: **A GREEN TEST CERTIFIED DEAD CODE, and that is the finding.** The test was named "PERMITS a project that predates the registration hook (compatibility mode)" and its own comment said refusing those 62 "would break every legacy build". It passed because its `Project.findByPk` mock returned a plain object literal carrying `created_at` — **the shape of the TypeScript declaration, not of the runtime instance**. `declare created_at: Date` emits no runtime property and `created_at` is not in the `init()` attribute block, so with `timestamps/underscored` the attribute is `createdAt`. The mock encoded the same false belief as the type, and a fixture built from the type cannot discover that the type is a lie — the more faithfully it matches the declaration, the more certainly it passes. Deepest instance of this run's recurring class: not an unchecked instrument, but one built from the same mistaken premise as the thing it measures. **WHAT P8-T4 ACTUALLY TURNS ON, corrected:** with the fix, arming the gate permits the 62 legacy projects and **still refuses the 10 post-hook ones** as `blueprint_not_approved` — correct by design (all 10 sit at `discovery` with prerequisites "not assessed", and `not_assessed` BLOCKS under the NOT_MEASURED doctrine), but it means arming it stops story generation for every current and new student project until a blueprint is approved through the product. **That is a product decision for the DRI, not the one-line edit three documents describe.** **SYSTEMIC, ESCALATED NOT FIXED:** **141 of 447 models** carry the same `declare created_at` shape with no matching attribute definition — each a place `tsc` accepts `.created_at` and hands back `undefined`. Most are harmless because nothing reads it that way (`StrategyCall` was checked and is safe, so `calendarController.ts:48` is not a second realized case). A model-layer fix touches a third of the models, which is CLAUDE.md ESCALATE tier; the cheap mitigation worth considering is a derived sweep test over `models/*.ts`, the same pattern `lifecycleRegistrationCoverage.test.ts` already uses.

The honest proxy is the interval to the first plan row. **SAFE REGARDLESS OF THE THRESHOLD:** the claim puts the whole decision in the WHERE clause (`status = 'generating' AND updated_at < cutoff`), so two racing callers cannot both win - Postgres applies the UPDATE once and the loser's predicate no longer holds.   - Notes: **ONE DISAGREEMENT, NOT TWO BUGS.** The instruction line was built from the FULL input while the body was sliced to `MAX_BLOCKS`, so the prompt ordered the model to cite blocks it had never been given - and `coversRequirementBlocks` found those ids in the instruction line and reported full coverage. Conflating them would make one unreportable, so the inverted corpus case states it outright rather than leaving it to be "simplified" away later. **The two RECORDED GAP cases are kept and inverted rather than deleted**, so the closure cannot silently regress, and the `SAFE-002 delimiters and grounding order survive` case still passes - the named trap was hardening overflow while weakening injection resistance in the same change. - [x] **The stale-build claim is no longer "argued from the SQL" - it is observed, and the mutation taught something**
  - Date: 2026-10-18
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `backend/src/services/sbp/__tests__/staleClaimRace.integration.test.ts` (new, 4 tests) and the register. No production code.
  - Verification: **STALE CLAIM RACE, against real Postgres.** PR #3169 recorded its own limit - the claim was *"argued from the SQL's atomicity, not observed"* - and added a seventh entry to this programme's list of locks covered only by SQL-text assertions. Closed for that one: two concurrent `claimStaleGeneratingIntake` calls on a stale row, **exactly one wins** and the row carries the winner's correlation id; a **fresh** row is claimed by nobody; a non-`generating` row is never claimed; and the **mandatory positive control** - the read-then-write the implementation avoids, with both reads taken before either write - **produces two winners**, which is what makes the first result a measurement rather than an assertion. Ran against a disposable Postgres 16 on port 55434 (not 55433, which the agent-domain suite uses). Verified it SKIPS AND SAYS WHY without `DATABASE_URL`: 4 skipped, with the reason printed. Container removed afterwards.
  - Notes: **THE MUTATION TAUGHT SOMETHING I HAD NOT MEASURED.** Removing `AND updated_at < NOW() - ...` from the shipped statement fails **TWO** tests, not one. The fresh-row test fails as expected - but so does *exactly one wins*, because **both** callers then win: `status` is still `generating` for the loser, so nothing refuses it. That clause is therefore doing two jobs at once: it is the age gate AND it is the lock, because the winner bumping `updated_at` is what invalidates the loser's predicate. `planStore.ts` already said this in its docblock ("Moving `updated_at` is what claims it") - the difference is that it was reasoning then and is a measurement now. The practical consequence is worth the entry: anyone "simplifying" the claim to match on status alone would silently remove the mutual exclusion along with the age filter, and every existing unit test would still pass. **SIX REMAIN** - the execution-run claim, the room outbox, the scheduler claim, the enrollment row lock, the telemetry anchor and `resource_leases`. The pattern that closed this one is cheap and reusable, but it is one suite per lock and none of them run in CI until a pristine database can boot.

- [x] **`resource_leases` is the second of the seven locks to be observed rather than argued**
  - Date: 2026-10-18
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `backend/src/services/workGraph/__tests__/resourceLeaseRace.integration.test.ts` (new, 4 tests) and the register. No production code.
  - Verification: **RESOURCE LEASE RACE, against real Postgres.** This register had named `resource_leases` the closest structural analogue to the lock `lifecycleExecution` lacks and recorded that it had **no race test at all**. Now: two concurrent `acquireLease` calls on one `resource_key` grant **exactly one**, and the loser receives `conflictWith` rather than a database error; the **mandatory positive control** - the partial unique index dropped - grants **TWO**, which is what proves the INDEX refuses the second acquire rather than the application code; the same idempotency key twice is idempotent and short-circuits before any INSERT, asserted separately because it shares a return shape with losing a race; and a released lease frees the key, because the index only binds `active` rows. Disposable Postgres 16 on port **55435** (55433 is the agent-domain suite, 55434 the stale-claim suite, so all three can run together). Verified it SKIPS AND SAYS WHY without `DATABASE_URL`. Container removed and its absence confirmed.
  - Notes: **A DIFFERENT MECHANISM FROM THE ONE PROVED ALONGSIDE IT.** The stale-build claim is a compare-and-swap in a WHERE clause; this is a partial unique index. Both were argued from their SQL and both are now measured, which is worth noting because the same word - "lock" - was covering two unrelated things in the register. **The control is the whole test.** Removing the index and watching two leases appear is what distinguishes "Postgres refuses this" from "our code happens not to try twice". Risk R5 found precisely that shape in `factoryApproval`: a compare-and-swap that refused nobody, with the unique index as the only real backstop. The generalisation is worth keeping: an application-level check wrapped around a database constraint is usually decoration, and the only way to find out is to remove the constraint. **FIVE REMAIN** - the execution-run claim, the room outbox, the scheduler claim, the enrollment row lock, the telemetry anchor. The pattern is established twice over now and costs about an hour each. **But three such suites now exist that only a developer with Docker will ever run**, which is better than nothing and worse than a gate; the ordering that fixes that is already recorded - a pristine database has to boot before CI can run any of them.

- [x] **The scheduler's documented lock does not exist; the real one is an undocumented predicate on a path that emails real people**
  - Date: 2026-10-18
  - Session: CC-20261001-q7m4
  - What changed: `backend/src/services/schedulerService.ts` - comment only, +20/-2, describing the mechanism that is actually there; plus the register.
  - Verification: **SCHEDULER LOCK, and there was nothing to prove.** `schedulerService.ts:425` said the claim was made "using FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED". Measured: that phrase occurs **exactly once in the entire file, in that comment** - `grep -n "FOR UPDATE\|SKIP LOCKED"` returns one line, and it is the comment. The claim is a lock-free `SELECT` of candidate ids followed by `UPDATE ... WHERE id IN (:ids) AND status = 'pending' RETURNING *`. **The real protection is that predicate:** the second instance blocks on the first's row locks and, under READ COMMITTED, re-evaluates its WHERE once they are released, by which time the rows read `processing`, so it matches none. Sound - but not what was documented, and the same compare-and-swap shape as the stale-build claim rather than a queue lock. Backend `tsc`: 4 errors, all pre-existing `playwright`. **Two inventory suites fail, and I PROVED they are pre-existing rather than assuming it:** parked my edited file, restored the committed version, re-ran `generateReese*` - **identical 2 failed / 8 passed** - then restored my copy and confirmed the comment and the CRLF endings survived.
  - Notes: **A COMMENT NAMING A MECHANISM THAT IS NOT PRESENT IS WORSE THAN NO COMMENT**, because it makes the real one look like leftover defensive code. **Three sender instances run on production.** `AND status = 'pending'` looks redundant - the rows were just selected as pending - and deleting it would leave every existing test passing while three senders claimed the same rows and emailed real people twice. Same class as `coversRequirementBlocks` checking the instruction line instead of the body, and the `legacy_compatibility` guard reading a property that was always `undefined`: documentation and behaviour drifted, and only the documentation was being read. **NOT OBSERVED, DELIBERATELY:** proving it needs the claim exercised without the send path, i.e. extracting that block into an exported `claimPendingActions(processorId)` while preserving the transaction and the empty-candidates early return with its heartbeat. Restructuring a live email scheduler is not a comment fix, so it is proposed in the register rather than bundled here. **FOUR REMAIN:** the execution-run claim, the room outbox, the enrollment row lock, the telemetry anchor.

The constant being exported is good; the conclusion drawn from it was not. **NOTHING EXECUTES IT IN PRODUCTION YET:** the only mentions are the definition, a doc reference, and that text assertion, so this is proven *before* it has a caller - the right order - but a green run here is **not** evidence that any queue is running.
